Action Replay codes · mGBA

Pokémon Generation 3 Evolved Field Guide

Cheat codes for the ROM hack Pokémon Generation 3 Evolved (versions 1.0.20, 1.0.24 and 1.0.26), worked out from the game itself and tested in the mGBA emulator, plus look-ups for its items, moves, abilities, every species (with sprites, base stats and evolutions) and the wild Pokémon of Kanto, the Sevii Islands and Hoenn.

Your game version:

Every code on this page switches to match, on every tab. Not sure? In mGBA open File → ROM info…: CRC32 is 122351e1 on 1.0.26, 64eac717 on 1.0.24 and a459e886 on 1.0.20. Each version's codes work only on that version: the game's code moved between them, so a code made for another version does nothing or stops the game (tested). Saves carry over (tested from 1.0.20 to 1.0.24 and from 1.0.24 to 1.0.26, and back); codes you added for an older version need adding again, copied from this page with your new version picked. The party editor (Your Pokémon) is for 1.0.26 only.

Using a code in mGBA

  1. Open the cheats window. With the game running in mGBA, pick Tools → Cheats…
  2. Paste it and add it. Press Copy on a code in this guide, paste it into the box that says Enter codes here… and click Add New Code. Leave the code type on Autodetect.
  3. Tick it on, untick it off. A new code starts ticked, so it's already working. Untick it to switch it off. Double-click its name, (untitled), to rename it.

Before you start

  • Check it's the right game file. In mGBA, with the game running, open File → ROM info…: CRC32 should say a459e886 (1.0.20) or 64eac717 (1.0.24) or 122351e1 (1.0.26). Pick that version in the bar at the top of the page. Anything else is a different version of Pokémon Generation 3 Evolved: the codes won't work, and the wrong version's codes could crash the game.
  • Back up your save. Copy the .sav file next to your ROM somewhere safe before trying codes. Every code here was tested for save damage, but a backup costs nothing.
  • Add each code on its own so you can switch them on and off separately. Don't add a "master code": mGBA doesn't need one, and one made for another game stops these codes working. If you already added one, remove all your cheats and restart mGBA.
  • Codes only work while they're ticked. Whatever you get while one is on (items, money, Pokémon, moves) is normal game data, so it stays after you untick the code and save.
  • Untick a code before you remove it. If you press Remove on a code that's still ticked, its effect can keep running until you restart mGBA.
  • mGBA remembers your codes, and whether each one is ticked, the next time you open the game. Untick the ones you aren't using.
  • Your game file is never changed. Codes change the game only in mGBA's memory, and only while they're ticked.

What's different in this game

Generation 3 Evolved is built on pokeemerald-expansion (a modern Emerald decompilation with every generation's Pokémon, moves and abilities), set up as a FireRed game: you start in Kanto, then go on to the Sevii Islands and Hoenn. Its code and data sit in different places from Emerald's, FireRed's and other expansion hacks', so use only the codes on this page with it.

  • Every generation's Pokémon. 1575 species entries, 555 of them forms (Alolan, Galarian, Hisuian and Paldean forms, Megas, Gigantamax and more). The Pokédex tab lists them all, with sprites.
  • TMs teach through the storage screen. Using a TM (or talking to a move tutor) opens the Pokémon storage screen, where you pick who learns it, and TMs aren't used up. Any Pokémon can learn any TM covers the tutors too.
  • No PC item storage. This game's save has no room for a PC item box, so Any item ×999 puts the items straight into your Bag when you open it.
  • Natures, three abilities and hidden ones. 1280 species have a hidden ability, and wild Pokémon sometimes come with it on their own; Pick the ability makes it every time.
  • Roaming legendaries. 39 Pokémon (legendaries, Mythicals and Ultra Beasts) can roam; some keep to a few places, which the Encounter map lists, and each one's card says it roams and what sets it loose (a Gym badge, catching another legendary, a full Kanto Pokédex).
  • Seven Bag pockets: Items, Medicine, Berries, TMs & HMs, Poké Balls, Power Ups, Key Items.

To check the SHA-1 without mGBA: on Windows, PowerShell Get-FileHash -Algorithm SHA1 "Generation 3 Evolved.gba"; on macOS or Linux, shasum "Generation 3 Evolved.gba".

How the codes were made and tested

Every code was worked out from this ROM. The expansion's source (version 1.16.3, built as FireRed) was compiled for reference, and its functions were matched to the ROM's by their instructions, which found most of the game's functions and tables; the rest were found from the code that uses them. Each code changes the spot where the game decides a thing (a new Pokémon's personality and ability, the money it stores, the move a TM teaches…) by patching instructions in mGBA's copy of the ROM. The catching codes and the item code add a small routine of their own in the ROM's empty space; the game still builds every Pokémon and item itself, checksum and all.

Every code was then run in a headless build of mGBA 0.10.5, driving the game like a player: walks through the grass, the game's own event scripts, a Poké Mart, the Bag, the storage screen, real battles and real Poké Ball throws. After each test the result was read from the game's memory, and anything saved was checked by saving with the codes on, unticking everything, resetting and pressing Continue: the Pokémon, items and money had to come back unchanged, with a valid checksum, no Bad Egg, and the game loading straight into the overworld. The ROM file's SHA-1 was the same after all testing.

The details for each code, with the tables and screenshots, are in the Under the hood section at the bottom of its tab. These checks cover every code at once:

Version 1.0.26: what changed for the codes

1.0.26 (with 1.0.25's changes) is the whole game built again too, and every function the codes patch moved once more: from 1.0.20's places by 0x94 to 0x780 bytes. None of 1.0.24's codes can be used on 1.0.26: of the 130 ROM patches in a cheat file of 1.0.24 codes from this page, none lands on the instruction it was made for in 1.0.26 (tested). Pick 1.0.26 at the top of the page and every code switches to its build for 1.0.26.

The release notes (1.0.25: fixes for MyBoy, NPC and trade bugs, VS Seeker in Lavaridge Gym, tiles, Drought only by day, Soothe Bells in Celadon, level-based evolutions for the Pokémon that evolve by friendship; 1.0.26: player sprite fixes, a Battle Factory softlock fix, a Doubles toggle) describe nothing aimed at what the codes change. In the ROM, the ball-throw command is 1.0.24's, unchanged, 0xE8 bytes earlier, so Catch on the first ball is the same one-instruction change there. Some of the game's RAM moved (EWRAM from 0x020348C0 up, by 0x14 bytes), none that a code uses: the save blocks, the party and the Battle Points stay where they were. The look-ups are read from 1.0.26: 34 Pokémon that evolve by friendship can also evolve at a level, 5 learnsets gained moves, Celadon's Game Corner sells its TMs for 500 coins and a Soothe Bell, Pallet Town's Old Rod catches Arrokuda, and Shaymin roams at Lv30. Every other look-up is the same as 1.0.24's.

The addresses were found the same way as 1.0.24's (reloc.py: 99.1 % of the data lined up in runs that each moved as one; reloc.py check 1.0.26 compares the instructions around every address with 1.0.20's: all the same, five differences read by hand and found harmless), and every test on this page was run again on 1.0.26. The Your Pokémon tab (the party editor, EXP boost and Eggs hatch fast) and the Pokédex tab's Fill the Pokédex code are new in this guide with 1.0.26 and is built for 1.0.26 only.

Version 1.0.24: what changed for the codes

Each release of Generation 3 Evolved is the whole game built again from its source, so a change anywhere moves everything after it. Between 1.0.20 and 1.0.24 every function the codes patch moved, by 0x17C to 0x340 bytes. So none of 1.0.20's codes can be used on 1.0.24, and none of 1.0.24's on 1.0.20: every ROM patch would land on another instruction. Pick your version at the top of the page and every code switches to that version's.

1.0.24's release notes describe a fix for some Pokémon escaping a Master Ball and having much lower catch odds. In the ROM, the start of the ball-throw command was rewritten for it. That command ends in the same check Catch on the first ball changes (cmp r7, #0xFE / bhi), with the same shake roll after it, so the 1.0.24 code is the same change at the new address. Celadon's Game Corner also gained two prizes, TM67 and TM84. Nothing else the look-ups read changed: the items, moves, abilities, every species' stats, abilities, evolutions and learnsets, the wild tables, sprites and the type chart are the same in both.

How the new addresses were found: the two ROMs were lined up by the blocks of bytes that are the same in both (reloc.py: 99.9 % of the data, in runs that each moved as one), which gives each address's place in 1.0.24. Then the instructions around every address a code uses were compared with 1.0.20's, calls and pointers included (reloc.py check), and the ball-throw command, which changed, was read by hand. The codes' own routines (the catching codes' and the item code's) sit in the ROM's empty space at the same place in both versions, assembled with each version's addresses. The money, coin and Battle Point locations in memory didn't move. Every code test on this page was then run again on 1.0.24 (1,126 tests, all passed; 1,126 on 1.0.20), from the same savestates made again for 1.0.24 by saving in the game on 1.0.20 and pressing Continue on 1.0.24.

Code formats

Encrypted
The codes you copy on this page are encrypted Action Replay (PAR v3) codes. mGBA reads them as Autodetect or Action Replay MAX.
Raw
Each code's raw (decrypted) form is in the Under the hood section of its tab, with the addresses it changes. Raw codes need Autodetect. Most are ROM patches: 00000000 18aaaaaa + 0000vvvv 00000000 puts the 16-bit value vvvv at address 0x08000000 + aaaaaa × 2.
Long codes
The species, nature, gender and ability codes are about 330 lines each, because each one carries the whole routine they share. Paste the whole code into one Add New Code. Ticking several of them at once is fine: they put the same routine in the same place, and mGBA keeps it while any of them is ticked.
In the browser
The page builds each code from your choices in codes.ts, a copy of the Python generator used for the tests. A check runs both on every code the page can make (300,243 codes, 6,351,995 lines: every species in each of the three versions, every nature, gender, ability and IV code, every TM and move pair, every item, and the fixed codes, for every game version, and over 3,000 party edits on 1.0.26), and they match exactly. That check runs in Node.js. The page itself was tested in Chromium at desktop and phone widths, not in other browsers.
Remove while ticked
Unticking a code restores the original instructions. Removing a code that's still ticked leaves its patch in mGBA's memory (tested), so untick first; restarting mGBA clears it.

Max money

Your money shows ₽99,999,999 while this is ticked.

7FB17058 2060025E 06BB4D2D 6D56AB30 B4B0DC87 3FE6D094 CF251D68 25BDFBC9 DF88AD50 56BBBD7B AD86124F 2823D8DA F62B5452 15F099E1 CF251D68 25BDFBC9 655AEEF9 BB6A3B1F BF0AC24E 5D03BE08 759E45E2 35E358A9 1B16D8B6 45682E58 4FAB85F5 54F15FFD AD86124F 2823D8DA E71B1756 F3868E35 CF251D68 25BDFBC9 35ECF878 BE480046 CF251D68 25BDFBC9

How to use: tick it, then buy something once in a Poké Mart. That saves ₽99,999,999 in your game, so you can untick it.

  • Untick it before any money changes hands and your real amount comes back.
  • While it's ticked, shops don't take your money.

Max coins

Your Game Corner coins show 9,999 while this is ticked (you need the Coin Case).

1481EE4C C4852B69 0EC2670E 231988CE 71BD0A41 51326E02 CF251D68 25BDFBC9 DA91294A F15A36EC 9CF22116 6A8BAB19 B655CD12 D679E90F AA7C07D3 0D67D1F8 C4FE0350 10356007 73ECD9F4 BF3167A8 ED5E5AE1 8C178CC5 2A317183 B89D3060 2C093580 EE50BE55 0AD24176 0ACC68BE

How to use: tick it, then get or spend coins once. The game then saves 9,999 itself, so you can untick it.

  • Untick it before any coins change hands and your real count comes back.
  • While it's ticked, spending coins doesn't take any.

Max Battle Points

Sets your Battle Points to 9,999.

9E744D4C 7E0AE1B5

How to use: tick it for a moment, then untick it. The 9,999 stays; save the game to keep it.

  • While it's ticked, your BP are set back to 9,999 all the time, so the Battle Frontier's prizes cost nothing.

Any item, ×999

Tops any item up to 999 in your Bag. Find the item below, press Copy code on its row and add the code.

Press Where on a row to see where the game has that item: Found (item balls), Hidden, Given (someone hands it to you), Shops, the Game Corner and BP exchange prize counters with their prices, and Wild held (wild Pokémon that may hold it, with the chance).

How to use: tick the code, then open the Bag from the Start menu: the item is there, ×999, in its own pocket. Then untick the code.

  • It only acts when the Bag is opened from the Start menu (not from a battle or a shop).
  • If you already have some, it adds the rest; if the pocket is full, nothing is added.
  • What it adds stays after you untick it.
  • Shops: most in Hoenn means at least half of that region's shops sell it, so any Poké Mart there likely has it.
  • The places are the game's own place names, so a cave's floors or a city's buildings share one name. Berry trees, Pickup and items a script picks at random aren't listed, and an item with none of these sources has no Where.
  • The icon beside each name is the one the game shows for that item in the Bag.
Hex IDDecItemPocketCode
No items match that search.
Under the hoodHow the item, money, coin and BP codes work, and how they were tested

Bag item code

The raw forms below are 1.0.24's. The addresses in the text are 1.0.20's, where the codes were first worked out: in 1.0.24 the same code sits up to 0x340 bytes further on, and every address the 1.0.24 codes use is listed on the Start here tab (Under the hood, "Version 1.0.24").

The raw forms below are 1.0.26's. The addresses in the text are 1.0.20's, where the codes were first worked out: in 1.0.26 the same code sits 0x94 to 0x780 bytes further on, and every address the 1.0.26 codes use is listed on the Start here tab (Under the hood, "Version 1.0.26").

IIIIItem hex ID from the list, always 4 digits (Rare Candy = 0066).

This is the raw (decrypted) form, so it needs Autodetect. The codes in the list are the same lines, encrypted.

Why not a RAM write
This game has no PC item storage (its save keeps no PC item slots), and the Bag's quantities are stored XOR'd with the save's key (SaveBlock2 + 0xB0), so writing a number into memory wouldn't give a valid item.
0x08DA3C4C
The Start menu's BAG entry: a pointer to StartMenuBagCallback (0x0821AE75), which the menu calls every frame until the screen has faded out. The code points it at a routine in the ROM's empty space (asm/bag_item.s, 60 bytes at 0x09FF8000, the item ID right after it).
The routine
Asks the game's CountTotalItemQuantityInBag (0x08170454) how many there are, and if fewer than 999, gives the rest with AddBagItem (0x081706A4), which puts them in the item's own pocket, key and all (and does nothing if the pocket is full). Then it runs the real callback, so the Bag opens as usual.
0x08778E04
Item table (gItemsInfo): 901 entries of 48 bytes; names and pockets on this page come from it. The 882 items listed are the ones with a name.
The routine's source (asm/bag_item.s)
@ Generation 3 Evolved: the routine behind "Any item x999" (assembled at BAG_BASE; tests.py asm checks it). @ @ The code points the Start menu's BAG entry (its callback pointer at 0x08DA3C4C, normally StartMenuBagCallback @ 0x0821AE75) at this routine. Each time the entry runs (it is called once a frame until the screen has faded) the @ routine tops the item up to 999 with the game's own functions, CountTotalItemQuantityInBag (0x08170455) and @ AddBagItem (0x081706A5, which puts it in its own pocket and refuses when the pocket is full), then hands over to @ the real callback, so the Bag opens as usual with the item in it. The item ID is the halfword right after the @ routine (ITEM). @ COUNT, ADDBAG and BAG_CALLBACK are given by codes.py (BAG_ASM, through at() for the version being built); the @ addresses in these comments are 1.0.20's. .global G3E_BagItem G3E_BagItem: push {r4, lr} adr r4, item ldrh r0, [r4] bl count ldr r1, =999 cmp r0, r1 bhs 1f subs r1, r1, r0 ldrh r0, [r4] bl addbag 1: pop {r4} pop {r0} mov lr, r0 ldr r0, =BAG_CALLBACK bx r0 count: ldr r3, =COUNT bx r3 addbag: ldr r2, =ADDBAG bx r2 .ltorg .balign 4 item:

The item icons

Each name in the list has the icon the game draws for that item in the Bag, read from 1.0.26 by extract_item_icons.py (the addresses here are 1.0.26's).

0x081716D8, 0x08171744
GetItemIconPic and GetItemIconPalette, the game's own choice of picture and palette for an item, run in mGBA for every item: the item table's picture and palette (+0x24, +0x28), except TMs and HMs, which share one disc picture each, coloured by their move's type.
The pictures
24 × 24 pixels, 16 colours, compressed; the game's own routine unpacks them. An item with no picture of its own gets the game's question mark (item 0's); none of the items in the list needs it.

Tested in mGBA with no codes (tests_item_icons.py): for every item in the list, the icon the Bag puts on screen (AddItemIconSprite, 0x081717C8, read back from the screen's sprite memory) is the same as this page's, pixel for pixel.

Where each item is found

Read from the ROM by extract_item_sources.py: every map's people, signs, step triggers and map scripts, followed through their calls and branches the way the game runs them.

Found
Item balls: people on a map whose script is the common item-ball script (0x08389FF8). It asks the game for the item the ball carries (callnative 0x08171141, the object's u16 at +0x0E) and gives it with finditem.
Hidden
Background events of kind 7: a 32-bit word with the item (bits 0–10), its flag (11–23), how many (24–30) and whether it's buried underfoot (bit 31, found only with the Itemfinder). Item 0 is the Game Corner's hidden coins (left out).
Given
giveitem / finditem (callstd 0 and 1 with the item in VAR 0x8000), FireRed's giveitem_msg (callstd 11) and additem (0x44), with the variables a script sets before a branch carried into it. One that first takes money (removemoney: the vending machines, the Mt. Chimney cookie stand, the Route 109 seashore house) is listed under Shops.
Shops
pokemart (0x86), a list of items ending in 0, and the hack's second kind of mart (0x87: a list ending in 0, then the clerk's own message), used by the counters that sell vitamins, Power items, Incenses, Scarves, Berries and Flutes. An item at least half of a region's shops sell is “most in” that region (Hoenn 14, Kanto 10, the Sevii Islands 5 shops), the rest by place (the 0x87 counters don't count as shops for that).
Game Corner, BP exchange
Command 0x88 (Mauville and Celadon's prize counters) and the hack's own 0xE7 (the Battle Frontier's exchange): lists of item and price (coins or BP) ending in 0.
Wild held
Every species in a wild table: its two held items in gSpeciesInfo (+0x0E, 50 %; +0x10, 5 %; 100 % when both are the same item).
Not listed
Berry trees, Pickup, items a script picks into a variable (prizes drawn at random, the Lilycove lottery), the Battle Pyramid's and Trainer Hill's floors (no place name), and Pokémon you're given holding an item.

Tested in the game with no codes (tests_items.py): every item ball (485 walked up to and picked up, 3 the test save can't walk up to run with their own script); 268 hidden items picked up beside them; every gift whose item is in its own commands run as copied from its script; every shop list opened with its own command, its first and last item bought (coins and BP taken checked against the prices); wild held items in Route 1's grass and in battles with a Zigzagoon, a Miltank and a Shuckle.

TestResult
The 488 item balls: warp beside each, turn to it, press A: the item goes into the Bag (4 runs)PASS
The 283 hidden items: beside each, turn to it, press A: the item goes into the Bag (the ones the test save can't reach are listed, not checked) (2 runs)PASS
The 212 scripted gifts and purchases whose item is in their own commands (giveitem, finditem, additem): those commands, copied from the map script, run in the game put the item in the Bag (64 whose item a script picks into a variable are left out) (2 runs)PASS
The 64 shop lists (Poké Marts, the Game Corner prize counters, the BP exchange), each opened with its own command from the map script: the first and the last item bought through the game's menus; coins and BP taken are the prices on the pagePASS

The places are 1.0.26's, tested on 1.0.26. The older versions differ only at Celadon's Game Corner prize counter: 1.0.24 charges 1,000 coins for its TMs and has no Soothe Bell (1.0.25 added it, 500 coins), and 1.0.20 also lacks TM67 and TM84 there. (The 0x87 counters were first read on 1.0.26; they are in 1.0.20 and 1.0.24 too.)

Money, coins and Battle Points

Money (SaveBlock1 + 0x490) and coins (+0x494) are stored XOR'd with the save's key (SaveBlock2 + 0xB0), so a plain memory write can't set them. The codes change the game's own money and coin functions instead, and the game stores the maximum itself the next time it adds or takes some, key and all.

Money
GetMoney (0x08193F40) returns 99,999,999 (the literal already in AddMoney's pool); IsEnoughMoney (0x08193F68) and the "enough for the cost in var 0x8005?" special (0x08193FC4) always say yes; AddMoney (0x08193F80) and RemoveMoney (0x08193FA8) both branch to AddMoney's "cap at 99,999,999" store; the "take the cost in var 0x8005" special (0x08193FF0) returns at once.
Coins
GetCoins (0x080FA01C) returns 9,999; AddCoins (0x080FA06C, 0x080FA07A) stores 9,999; RemoveCoins (0x080FA0CE–0x080FA0D2) stores 9,999.
Battle Points
SaveBlock2 + 0x9E8, a plain u16. One type 0x42 line writes 9,999 there through gSaveBlock2Ptr (0x030052DC) every frame.

Which new Pokémon should the IV, species, nature, gender and ability codes change?

Only wild Pokémon: the ones in the grass, caves and water, on a fishing rod or behind a Rock Smash rock.Wild Pokémon, plus the one-off Pokémon you walk up to and battle: Mewtwo, the legendary birds, Snorlax, the Regis, Kyogre and Groudon, Mew, Lugia, Ho-Oh…Wild Pokémon, one-off battles, and Pokémon the game gives you: your starter, the fossils, Eevee, Lapras, Hitmonlee or Hitmonchan, the Paradox Pokémon, Magearna… This is the one most people want. The codes below switch to match. Always shiny already covers all of these, and Eggs.

New Pokémon get their species' hidden ability: wild Pokémon.wild Pokémon and one-off battles.wild Pokémon, one-off battles and gifts. The Pokédex tab lists each species' three abilities.

  • A species with no second or hidden ability gets its first one, as the game does.
  • This game gives some wild Pokémon their hidden ability on its own; with this code ticked, every one gets the ability you picked instead.

60A9130A 322E56F5 42A3F330 D65EE38A

Any Poké Ball you throw at a wild or one-off Pokémon catches it on the first try, even at full HP, like a Master Ball.

  • The ball is used up as usual.

5DADBA98 C4BFEAEB 8E883EFF 92E9660D FBB4B78B E195F83B CF251D68 25BDFBC9

No random battles in grass, caves or water, and none behind Rock Smash rocks.

  • Fishing and one-off battles still work.

C36CFAE1 9B682813 CDE8AE47 E0DF7372 A87E5182 A329D9A8 CDE8AE47 E0DF7372 F3CDDA7C 2B78BED4 CDE8AE47 E0DF7372 00DDD171 1FEADE9F CDE8AE47 E0DF7372

On any route, cave or lake, the DexNav shows every Pokémon that lives there instead of a question mark, even the ones your Pokédex hasn't seen: its icon, name, types, search level and the items it can hold. A searches for any of them and R registers any of them. For version 1.0.26.

How to use: tick the code and open DexNav from the Start menu as usual (Professor Oak gives it to you).

  • It only changes what the DexNav shows while it's ticked: nothing goes into your save and your Pokédex isn't changed. Untick it and Pokémon you haven't seen are question marks again. A Pokémon you meet is marked seen the usual way.
  • The Hidden Ability line still says Capture first! until you catch that Pokémon, as in the game.
  • A Pokémon you register with R stays registered after you untick the code: R on the map still searches for it.
  • The game finds water Pokémon only while you're surfing: pick one from land and it says it can't be found nearby.
  • Pokémon you find this way get Always shiny, Perfect IVs, Pick the nature and Pick the gender like any wild Pokémon. The DexNav sets their ability itself, so Pick the ability doesn't change them; with Pick the species ticked you meet the species you picked instead.
  • Searches still work with No random encounters ticked.
  • To have Pokémon marked seen for good, use Fill the Pokédex.
  • Made for 1.0.26 only.
Under the hoodHow the catching codes work in memory, and how they were tested

How each code works

The raw forms below are 1.0.24's. The addresses in the text are 1.0.20's, where the codes were first worked out: in 1.0.24 the same code sits up to 0x340 bytes further on, and every address the 1.0.24 codes use is listed on the Start here tab (Under the hood, "Version 1.0.24").

The raw forms below are 1.0.26's. The addresses in the text are 1.0.20's, where the codes were first worked out: in 1.0.26 the same code sits 0x94 to 0x780 bytes further on, and every address the 1.0.26 codes use is listed on the Start here tab (Under the hood, "Version 1.0.26").

Raw forms need Autodetect. For the codes that come in versions, the raw form shown is the version picked at the top of this tab. In the raw templates, the highlighted value is the one you'd change.

Always shiny

00000000 180E5E7B 00002001 00000000 00000000 180E5E7C 000046C0 00000000 00000000 18106EA0 00004280 00000000
00000000 180E5FB9 00002001 00000000 00000000 180E5FBA 000046C0 00000000 00000000 18106FEA 00004280 00000000
00000000 180E6053 00002001 00000000 00000000 180E6054 000046C0 00000000 00000000 181070D6 00004280 00000000

This game decides a Pokémon's shininess once, when it's made, and stores it with the Pokémon (a "shiny" bit in its data, next to the usual shiny value). CreateBoxMon (0x081CBB20) makes every Pokémon; for one with you as its original trainer (wild, one-off battles, gifts, Eggs) it asks ComputePlayerShinyOdds (0x081C4328: the shiny odds, the Shiny Charm and the chain rerolls) at 0x081CBCF6. The first two patches turn that call into movs r0, #1 / nop: "yes". A gift's script can also set shininess itself after the Pokémon is made (always, never, or the usual roll); the third patch (0x0820DD40, cmp r7, #0 → cmp r0, r0) makes that "always". Trainers' Pokémon and in-game trades take other branches. The personality isn't touched.

00000000 18122C08 0000211F 00000000 00000000 18106D16 0000211F 00000000 00000000 18106D65 0000211F 00000000 00000000 18106D8C 0000211F 00000000 00000000 180E65D2 000039E0 00000000 00000000 1810710E 0000231F 00000000 00000000 1810710F 0000803B 00000000 00000000 18107110 0000E010 00000000 00000000 1810711F 0000231F 00000000 00000000 18107120 0000803B 00000000 00000000 18107121 000046C0 00000000

Wild Pokémon and one-off battles are made with CreateMonWithIVs (0x081CBD7C) and "fixed IV" 32, which means random; any value below 32 fills every IV with it. The code changes that 32 (movs r1, #0x20) to 31 at each call its version covers: CreateWildMon (0x0824534C), the setwildbattle command's single and double battles (0x0820D5C0, 0x0820D65E, 0x0820D6AC) and the legendaries' event special 0x201 (0x081CC7F4, where the 32 is built as 0xFF − 0xDF: the code makes it − 0xE0). Gifts (givemon) write each IV after the Pokémon is made (random unless the script sets one); the gift version turns the loop's two IV checks (0x0820DDB0, 0x0820DDD2) into "write 31".

The routine the species, nature, gender and ability codes share

These four codes add the same routine (asm/catch.s, 296 bytes at 0x09FF0000, in the ROM's empty space). It has two entry points, and each code adds the jump to the one it needs (ldr r4, [pc] / bx r4 and the address, in place of a few of the game's instructions, which the routine runs itself before jumping back):

  • 0x081CBB22, the start of CreateBoxMon: the species, nature and gender (species, nature and gender codes).
  • 0x081CBCB4, where CreateBoxMon sets the ability number (ability code).

Markers. CreateBoxMon is told whose Pokémon it is making by a "trainer ID" argument: 0 means you. A marker changes that 0, at one call, to 3 (wild), 4 (one-off) or 5 (gift); CreateBoxMon still treats those as you. The routine acts only on a call carrying a marker, and reads a small table at 0x09FF0400, one slot for each kind. The Wild only version marks CreateWildMon's call (0x0824534A)Wild + one-off version marks CreateWildMon's call (0x0824534A), the setwildbattle command's three (0x0820D5BE, 0x0820D65A, 0x0820D6AA) and the legendaries' event special 0x201 (0x081CC7CA)broadest version marks those and the givemon command's call (0x0820DD2C) and fills the wild slotthose two slotsall three slots.

What the routine does (each table entry 0xFFFF = not asked): a species replaces the one passed in, so everything after (the Pokédex, the level, the game's own rules) sees your pick; for nature and gender it searches for a personality that has the ones asked for and keeps the one not asked for from the game's own first draw, keeping the personality's shiny value in the same block of 8, so a Pokémon that would have been shiny still is and one that wouldn't still isn't; the ability is stored as the ability number asked for, after the game has set its own. Species that are always one gender or genderless skip the gender test. The ability code also turns off two places that set the ability number again later: CreateWildMon's own hidden-ability roll (0x0824536C) and, in the gift version, a gift script's ability (0x0820DE0C). The game then builds the Pokémon itself, checksum and all.

All four codes start with the same 296 lines, the routine:

The routine's source (asm/catch.s)
@ Generation 3 Evolved: the routine behind the catching codes (species, nature, gender, ability). @ Assembled at CATCH_BASE (codes.py); tests.py asm checks that the bytes codes.py stores still match this source. @ @ CreateBoxMon(boxMon r0, species r1, level r2, personality r3, [sp] trainerId.method, [sp+4] trainerId.value) at @ 0x081CBB20. trainerId.method is 0 for the player's own Pokemon, 1 for a preset OT (in-game trades), 2 for a random @ OT that's never shiny (trainers); CreateBoxMon treats anything else like 0. The codes mark the calls they cover by @ passing 3 (wild), 4 (one-off battle) or 5 (gift) where the game passes 0, so the Pokemon is still the player's. @ @ Table: one 16-byte slot per kind of call, TBL + 16 * (method - 3). Each entry is a halfword (so different codes @ never write the same halfword) and 0xFFFF, what free space reads, means "not set": @ +0 species +4 nature (0-24) +6 gender (0 male, 0xFE female) +8 ability (0 first, 1 second, 2 hidden) @ A code's versions write their entry into the wild slot, the wild and one-off slots, or all three. @ @ Hook A, CreateBoxMon's prologue (0x081CBB22-0x081CBB2B become ldr r4,[pc,#4] / bx r4 / nop / .word G3E_CatchHook): @ species: replaced with the entry. Nature / gender: when either entry is set, the Pokemon keeps its own @ personality if that already fits; otherwise the routine draws new ones until one does: the nature (personality % @ 25) and the gender (low byte against the species' gender ratio; skipped for single-gender and genderless species, @ so it can't loop forever). What no entry asks for keeps the first personality's value (so Synchronize and Cute @ Charm still work). Every draw keeps the "shiny value" (high half ^ low half) in the same block of 8, so the @ game's shiny odds are untouched (tests.py asm checks every nature can still come up that way). @ Hook B, where CreateBoxMon sets the ability number (0x081CBCB4-0x081CBCBB become ldr r4,[pc,#0] / bx r4 / .word @ G3E_AbilityHook): for a marked call with an ability entry, the ability number is the entry; otherwise exactly @ what the game does (personality bit 0, only for species with a second ability). .equ TBL, CATCH_TABLE @ The game's addresses (GENDER_RATIOS, RANDOM32, UMOD, SANITIZE, RESUME, SET_ABILITY, EPILOGUE) are given by codes.py @ (CATCH_ASM, through at() for the version being built): the addresses in these comments are 1.0.20's. .global G3E_CatchHook G3E_CatchHook: ldr r4, [sp, #0x14] @ trainerId.method (after CreateBoxMon's push of five registers) subs r4, #3 cmp r4, #2 bls 0f b resume @ not a marked call 0: lsls r4, r4, #4 ldr r5, =TBL adds r5, r5, r4 @ r5 = this kind of call's slot ldrh r4, [r5, #0] adds r6, r4, #1 lsrs r6, r6, #16 @ 1 when the species entry is 0xFFFF bne 2f movs r1, r4 @ the species 2: push {r0-r3} @ species at sp+4, personality at sp+12 ldrh r0, [r5, #4] ldrh r1, [r5, #6] movs r3, r0 ands r3, r1 adds r3, #1 lsrs r3, r3, #16 beq 3f b done @ no nature or gender entry 3: sub sp, #12 @ [sp] nature, [sp+4] gender wanted, [sp+8] gender ratio ldr r6, [sp, #24] @ the first personality (the caller's) lsrs r2, r0, #8 beq 4f movs r0, r6 @ nature not asked for: the first personality's movs r1, #25 bl umod 4: str r0, [sp, #0] ldr r1, [sp, #16] @ species ldr r2, =0x108 muls r1, r2 ldr r2, =GENDER_RATIOS ldrb r1, [r1, r2] str r1, [sp, #8] subs r2, r1, #1 @ gender: 0 male, 1 female, 2 no test cmp r2, #0xFC bhi 72f @ single-gender or genderless ldrh r0, [r5, #6] lsrs r2, r0, #8 beq 71f lsls r0, r6, #24 @ not asked for: the first personality's (female when its low byte < ratio) lsrs r0, r0, #24 cmp r0, r1 sbcs r0, r0 negs r0, r0 b 73f 71: lsrs r0, r0, #7 @ asked: 0 -> 0 (male), 0xFE -> 1 (female) b 73f 72: movs r0, #2 73: str r0, [sp, #4] lsrs r7, r6, #16 @ r7 = shiny key: high half ^ low half of the first personality lsls r0, r6, #16 lsrs r0, r0, #16 eors r7, r0 movs r5, r6 @ candidate: the first personality check: movs r0, r5 movs r1, #25 bl umod ldr r1, [sp, #0] cmp r0, r1 bne next ldr r0, [sp, #4] cmp r0, #2 beq found lsls r1, r5, #24 lsrs r1, r1, #24 ldr r2, [sp, #8] cmp r1, r2 sbcs r1, r1 negs r1, r1 @ 1 when female cmp r0, r1 beq found next: bl random32 lsrs r4, r0, #16 @ new low half movs r1, #7 ands r0, r1 eors r0, r7 eors r0, r4 @ high half = low half ^ key ^ (0-7) lsls r0, r0, #16 orrs r0, r4 movs r5, r0 b check found: str r5, [sp, #24] @ the personality CreateBoxMon goes on with (r3 after the pop) add sp, #12 done: pop {r0-r3} resume: mov lr, fp @ CreateBoxMon's own prologue mov r7, sl mov r6, sb push {r6, r7, lr} mov lr, r8 ldr r4, =RESUME bx r4 random32: ldr r3, =RANDOM32 bx r3 umod: ldr r3, =UMOD bx r3 .global G3E_AbilityHook G3E_AbilityHook: ldr r4, [sp, #0x50] @ trainerId.method (CreateBoxMon's frame) subs r4, #3 cmp r4, #2 bhi 1f lsls r4, r4, #4 ldr r3, =TBL adds r3, r3, r4 ldrh r3, [r3, #8] @ the ability entry cmp r3, #2 bhi 1f @ 0xFFFF: not set str r3, [sp, #0x10] b set_ability 1: mov r0, sl @ what the game does: abilities[1] of SanitizeSpeciesId(species) bl sanitize lsls r3, r0, #5 adds r3, r3, r0 lsls r3, r3, #3 add r3, sb ldrh r3, [r3, #0x1a] cmp r3, #0 bne set_ability ldr r4, =EPILOGUE bx r4 set_ability: ldr r4, =SET_ABILITY bx r4 sanitize: ldr r3, =SANITIZE bx r3

Then each code's own lines: its jump, the markers for its version, then its table entries.

Names and stat effects are read from the ROM's nature table (gNaturesInfo, 0x08D7AC0C).

Which Pokémon each version covers

Wild only covers CreateWildMon (0x082452C4), which the grass, caves, water, fishing and Rock Smash all go through. Wild + one-off adds the setwildbattle command (single and double battles) and the event special 0x201 (CreateEnemyEventMon, 0x081CC770) that Mew, Deoxys, Lugia and Ho-Oh are made with. Wild, one-off + gifts adds the givemon command, which every gift uses: the starter, the fossils, the Paradox Pokémon and the rest. Eggs (except for Always shiny), trainers' Pokémon, in-game trades and the roaming Pokémon go through other calls; the roaming Pokémon weren't tested with these codes.

No random encounters

00000000 181225FA 00002000 00000000 00000000 181225FB 00004770 00000000
00000000 18122744 00002000 00000000 00000000 18122745 00004770 00000000
00000000 1812285C 00002000 00000000 00000000 1812285D 00004770 00000000

WildEncounterCheck (0x08244BF4) decides whether a step in grass, a cave or water (or a Rock Smash) starts a battle; the step routine and the Rock Smash routine are its only callers. The code makes it movs r0, #0 / bx lr: it always says no. Fishing and scripted battles don't go through it.

Catch on the first ball

00000000 1805A399 0000E01B 00000000
00000000 1805A457 0000E01B 00000000
00000000 1805A3E3 0000E01B 00000000

The ball-throw battle command (Cmd_handleballthrow) handles trainer battles and the Master Ball first, then works out the catch odds and at 0x080B4730 does cmp r7, #0xFE / bhi: above 254 it's caught at once, as with a Master Ball; otherwise it rolls the shakes. The code turns that bhi at 0x080B4732 into a plain b, so every throw is caught at once.

Show every Pokémon in the DexNav (1.0.26)

Show every Pokémon in the DexNav

00000000 1808C87C 0000E000 00000000 00000000 1808CEDC 0000E000 00000000 00000000 1808D0EE 0000E000 00000000 00000000 1808D2D1 0000E000 00000000

The DexNav is the expansion's (its dexnav.c), with the hidden-Pokémon row swapped for this hack's ITEMS box. A Pokémon the Pokédex hasn't seen is a question mark: each place that decides that calls SpeciesToNationalPokedexNum (0x081C83E4), then GetSetPokedexFlag(number, 0 = seen) (0x081B6E58), then cmp r0, #0. Four such calls decide everything the player sees and picks: DexNavGetSpecies (0x081190F8: the Pokémon under the cursor, which A searches and R registers), the slot icon (0x08119DB8), the info panel's name, types and search level (0x0811A1DC) and the ITEMS box (0x0811A5A2). The code turns the first half of each bl GetSetPokedexFlag into b +4 (0xE000), which skips the call: r0 keeps the Pokédex number, so "seen" becomes "has a Pokédex number". Every species in a wild table has one (tested); an empty slot (species 0) has none and stays empty. Only the first half of each bl is changed, so the code can be ticked at any moment without the second half running alone. The Pokédex, battles, the Hidden Ability line (a caught check) and the "caught them all" marks aren't touched, and nothing is written to the save.

Tested on 1.0.26 in mGBA from the test save after Professor Oak's own DexNav gift script. On every map with a land or water table, the DexNav was opened from the Start menu three times from the same moment: with the code; with no code and every Pokémon of the map marked seen in the Pokédex (how the game itself shows Pokémon you have seen); and with neither. Each time a picture was taken as it opened and with the cursor on each of the 17 slots. With the code every picture had to be the same, pixel for pixel, as the game's own for Pokémon you've seen (the Pokémon icons animate, and the picture may be a frame earlier or later: the comparison allows that), and the Pokédex had to be unchanged. Searches were played to the end: A on an unseen Pokémon, then walking up to it with A held (the DexNav's creeping) until the battle starts; walking through grass meets nothing by chance in these runs (a test-only patch), so the battle is the DexNav's.

TestResult
Every map with a land or water table (367): 6,606 pictures with the code, each the same as the game's own for Pokémon you've seen; 2,257 Pokémon unseen in the test save on 366 of the maps, question marks without the code (367 runs)PASS
TestResult
every species in a land or water table (659) has a National Pokédex number (the game's SpeciesToNationalPokedexNum, called for each); species 0, an empty slot, has nonePASS

Tested alone and together

Every combination

All 64 on/off combinations of the six codes (broadest versions), each with two sets of choices: Eevee, Modest, female, hidden ability (an Eevee is female one time in eight), and Ralts, Jolly, male, second ability. Every run made a Pokémon one of the four ways the game makes a new one: a wild battle in the grass, a one-off battle (setwildbattle), a double one-off battle and a gift (givemon). Each Pokémon was checked for species and level, shininess, all-31 IVs, nature, gender, ability, a valid checksum and no Bad Egg; with a code off, the species had to be the game's own. Each cell: runs.

The game's own one-off battles and gifts

A gift whose species comes from a choice (the starters, the fossils, the Paradox Pokémon, Hitmonlee or Hitmonchan) was run once for each species it can give. Without codes each Pokémon had to be what its script says; with all six it had to carry every code.

1. Which party slot?
Laid out like the game's POKéMON screen: slot 1 is the big box on the left (your lead, the one sent out first), and slots 2 to 6 are the rows on the right, top to bottom.

The code only changes that slot if this species is in it.

3. What to change

0–31 each
0–252 each, 510 in total

Any ability in the game, even one its species can't have. The Abilities tab lists them all.

0–255; 255 is the most

Changes one Pokémon you already have, for good. Pick the slot and the Pokémon in it, then what to change. For version 1.0.26.

Type the Pokémon in the slot first (step 2).

And keep this code ticked: Any ability (the same code for every Pokémon)

Pick the ability first.
  1. Copy the code and add it in mGBA.
  2. In the game, open the menu (Start) and pick POKéMON. The change is made as the party screen opens: check it on the Pokémon's summary (on the Skills page, A shows its IVs).
  3. Untick the code, then remove it.
  • While it's ticked, it runs every time you open POKéMON, but it only changes the slot while the species you typed is in it. If the party was reordered, the Pokémon evolved or the slot is empty, nothing changes.
  • The change stays after you untick. To change the Pokémon again, make a new code.
  • Ability: its first, second or hidden ability (a Pokémon without that one gets its first), or Any ability…: any of the game's abilities, even one its species can't have. That one is saved in the Pokémon itself, so it goes where the Pokémon goes: reorder the party, evolve, save and reload. The game uses it while a second code, Any ability, is ticked: one code, the same for every Pokémon you've given an ability (an Articuno with Snow Warning and a Zapdos with Drizzle need just the one). Untick it and those Pokémon have their hidden ability (their first, for a species without one) until you tick it again; nothing else changes and the save stays fine.
  • Tick only one party-edit code at a time. Eggs are left alone.
  • Made for 1.0.26 only: on an older version it would patch the wrong place, so this tab isn't shown for them.

1F74EFFF E1D48F92 159BBDDB CD366B0D A7C57C26 22B78B77 462D428E CC8B52BE D74724E2 F2F193AB D09E5EAB 1F6B7132 6A489A9D A37F0409 4D11E77F 24E295A7 C61F4C9F A849AECC 12640C20 1B1C41C7 86D28062 7243D9EE 5417C275 72476BA3 0C81767A BDD71A95 3D211952 DE92C25C AEE8CCE7 11CC77D1 0E660BB6 3369D2FF FF30BA7D 277685B7 F112488A 98547D80 C436C716 2FA56CBA 551129CE 1E30FCBF 10EDCFE5 82A0FEAB 65DA389A 09D8882C AD7F2CDD CDAB5505 9CF2F6F8 D4212764 445AB7B5 602A75AA BC51E511 49D43A23 C42B90FB F10CFE94 49373DC8 FCB4C699

Each Pokémon that gets EXP from a battle you win or a Pokémon you catch gets twice as much, while this is ticked.

  • It multiplies what the game gives each Pokémon: one that gets no EXP from a win still gets none.
  • With the Exp. Charm in your Bag, the code takes the charm's place: ×2 is twice the EXP, not three times.
  • One win or catch gives a Pokémon at most 457,151 EXP. That's what Max gives: a Lv5 Charmander reached level 100 in three wins.
  • A Pokémon at level 100 still gets none.
  • Tick only one EXP code at a time. EXP already gained stays after you untick.
  • Made for 1.0.26 only.

23F87ACD 2184A91B B2AECF49 E7D6CAED 0F2D3251 A183EA3E A229E43A 7722CE7C 5C887CED 8F216587 CDE8AE47 E0DF7372

An Egg in your party hatches within the first few steps.

  • The Egg has to be in your party, and you need to walk, as usual.
  • What hatches is a normal Pokémon: the code only speeds up the countdown.
  • Made for 1.0.26 only.

BF98521F C2D589FE 65DA389A 09D8882C 48FF7423 8AD65C58 B2AECF49 E7D6CAED 2F2AA529 28C373E9 EF1C599C 3B639AF5 30B1CAC5 DDE8DCEB 65DA389A 09D8882C

When a battle ends, each Pokémon in your party is holding the berry it had when the battle started, even if it was eaten, burnt by Incinerate, thrown with Fling, or eaten or taken by the other side's Bug Bite, Knock Off or Thief. For version 1.0.26.

  • Nothing changes during the battle: a berry still works once, as usual, and comes back when the battle is over, whether you win, run, catch the Pokémon or black out.
  • It works for every Pokémon in your party, in double battles too. A Pokémon that didn't battle keeps its berry.
  • If the Pokémon ends a wild battle holding another item (you Tricked the berry for the wild Pokémon's item), it keeps that item and the berry doesn't come back. In a trainer battle the game gives the trainer's item back, and the berry comes back.
  • In this game wild Pokémon can't take your Pokémon's items; trainers' Pokémon can.
  • Items that aren't berries already come back after a battle in this game, with or without the code. A Pokémon you catch keeps the item it was holding.
  • It only works while ticked: a berry used in a battle with the code unticked is gone. Berries that came back stay after you untick, and the code writes nothing else to your save.
  • Made for 1.0.26 only.
Under the hoodHow the party editor, Any ability, EXP, egg and berry codes work, and how they were tested

How each code works

Edit a party Pokémon

The ROM is empty (0xFF) from 0x09BC9098 to its end. The code writes a small routine at 0x09FF4000, between the catching codes' routine (0x09FF0000) and Any item ×999's (0x09FF8000), followed by a table: the species the code was made for (two bytes), the party slot, then one entry per field (the field, then the value as two bytes, low first), then 0. Its last two lines point the Start menu's POKéMON entry (the pointer at 0x08DA43C4, normally StartMenuPokemonCallback at 0x0821B311) at the routine. Any item ×999 uses the BAG entry next to it, so the two codes can be ticked together. Each time POKéMON is picked, the routine runs first: it reads the slot's species with the game's GetMonData (0x081C4E8C) and stops unless it's the one in the table (an empty slot reads as 0, so it never matches), skips an Egg, writes each field with the game's own SetMonData (0x081C6E48, which re-encrypts the Pokémon's data and fixes its checksum, so it stays valid), calls CalculateMonStats (0x081CB9F8), then jumps to the real callback and the party screen opens as usual. Unticking restores the pointer, so the routine is never reached again. The routine is Wishes of Tomorrow's guide's (both games are built on pokeemerald-expansion and number these fields the same way), plus one entry type of its own for Any ability (field 0xFF, below).

0x30–0x35
SetMonData fields for the IVs: HP, Attack, Defense, Speed, Sp. Atk, Sp. Def (the game's own order).
0x21–0x26
EVs, same order.
0x37
Ability number, two bits: 0 first, 1 second, 2 hidden, and 3 for Any ability (below). GetMonAbility (0x081CA90C) reads only this and the species; a species with no ability in that slot uses its first.
0xFF
Not a game field: the routine keeps an ability in the Pokémon's unused bits for Any ability (below).
0x0C
The nature used for stats: this build's Mint field. The game stores it as a 5-bit modifier XORed with the personality's nature (bits 3–7 of byte 0x12), so the personality, and with it the gender, stay as they were. This hack's summary shows this nature on the Skills page.
0x0B
Shiny: 1 or 0. It sets a shiny bit (bit 14 of the u16 at 0x1E) instead of changing the personality or trainer ID.
0x27
Friendship, 0–255.
0x2F
Poké Ball: 0 Strange Ball, 1 Poké Ball … 27 Cherish Ball (balls 1–27 are also their items' numbers).
0x12, 0x36
Species and "is an Egg", read with GetMonData for the checks.
How found
These field numbers differ from vanilla Emerald's in this build, so each was written on 1.0.26 in the emulator and the decrypted Pokémon compared before and after.
The routine (Thumb assembly, asm/party_editor.s)
@ Party editor (code "Edit a party Pokemon"), Generation 3 Evolved 1.0.26 and later only (the owner's call: new
@ features start on the latest version). The routine is Wishes of Tomorrow's (asm/party_editor.s there), with one
@ addition: field 0xFF, not a game field, keeps an ability in the Pokemon's own data for "Any ability" (setab, below;
@ read by the Any ability code, asm/any_ability.s). This build has the same SetMonData field IDs as Wishes of Tomorrow
@ (species 0x12, Egg 0x36; tested on 1.0.26, NOTES.md "Party editor").
@ Assembled at 0x09FF4000, in the ROM's free space (0xFF from 0x09FF0000 to the end; the catching routine is at
@ 0x09FF0000-0x09FF0430, the Bag routine at 0x09FF8000). The code points the Start menu's POKeMON entry (sStartMenuItems[1]
@ callback, the pointer at 0x08DA43C4 on 1.0.26) here. Each time the player picks POKeMON it applies the table to one
@ party slot with the game's own SetMonData (which re-encrypts the Pokemon and fixes its checksum), recalculates its
@ stats, then jumps to the real StartMenuPokemonCallback, so the party menu opens as usual.
@ table: species (u16, the Pokemon the code was made for), slot (0-5), then (field, value low byte, value high byte)
@ triples, then 0. SetMonData reads one byte for every field the editor sets; the high byte is 0. Field 0xFF's value
@ is the ability (both bytes); the code puts it before the ability number (0x37, value 3).
@ Safeguard: nothing happens unless the Pokemon in that slot is that species, so a code left ticked can't change a
@ different Pokemon after the party is reordered. Empty slots (species 0) and Eggs are skipped too.
@ Symbols (codes.py EDITOR_SYMBOLS): gPlayerParty, GetMonData, SetMonData, CalculateMonStats, StartMenuPokemonCallback.
editor:
    push {r4, r5, lr}
    adr r5, table
    ldrb r0, [r5, #2]          @ slot 0-5
    movs r1, #100
    muls r0, r1
    ldr r4, =gPlayerParty
    adds r4, r4, r0            @ r4 = &gPlayerParty[slot]
    movs r0, r4
    movs r1, #0x12             @ MON_DATA_SPECIES
    ldr r3, =GetMonData
    bl via
    ldrh r1, [r5]              @ the species the code was made for
    cmp r0, r1
    bne done                   @ a different Pokemon, or an empty slot: leave it alone
    movs r0, r4
    movs r1, #0x36             @ MON_DATA_IS_EGG
    ldr r3, =GetMonData
    bl via
    cmp r0, #0
    bne done                   @ leave Eggs alone
    adds r5, #3
loop:
    ldrb r1, [r5]              @ field; 0 ends the table
    cmp r1, #0
    beq stats
    cmp r1, #0xFF
    bne setmon
    ldrb r0, [r5, #1]
    ldrb r1, [r5, #2]
    lsls r1, r1, #8
    orrs r0, r1                @ the ability to keep
    bl setab
    b next
setmon:
    movs r0, r4
    adds r2, r5, #1            @ &value (low byte, then high byte)
    ldr r3, =SetMonData
    bl via
next:
    adds r5, #3
    b loop
stats:
    movs r0, r4
    ldr r3, =CalculateMonStats
    bl via
done:
    pop {r4, r5}
    pop {r3}
    mov lr, r3
    ldr r3, =StartMenuPokemonCallback
    bx r3
via:
    bx r3

@ setab(r4 = the Pokemon, r0 = an ability): keep the ability in the 9 bits this build never uses in the growth
@ substruct: word 0 bits 26-31 = its low 6 bits, word 1 bits 29-31 = its high 3. Only those two words are decrypted
@ (the key is personality XOR OT ID; the substructs' order is personality % 24), changed and encrypted again, and the
@ checksum (the sum of the 24 decrypted halfwords, at 0x1C) is corrected by the difference, so the Pokemon stays valid.
setab:
    push {r5, r6, r7, lr}
    movs r6, r0
    ldr r7, [r4]               @ personality
    ldr r5, [r4, #4]
    eors r5, r7                @ the key
    lsrs r0, r7, #3
    movs r1, #3
    svc #6                     @ BIOS Div: r1 = (personality >> 3) % 3
    lsls r1, r1, #3
    lsls r7, r7, #29
    lsrs r7, r7, #29
    adds r7, r7, r1            @ personality % 24
    adr r3, gpos
    ldrb r7, [r3, r7]
    adds r7, r4, r7            @ the growth substruct
    ldr r0, [r7]
    eors r0, r5                @ word 0
    ldr r1, [r7, #4]
    eors r1, r5                @ word 1
    ldrh r2, [r4, #0x1C]       @ the checksum, less the four old halfwords
    lsls r3, r0, #16
    lsrs r3, r3, #16
    subs r2, r2, r3
    lsrs r3, r0, #16
    subs r2, r2, r3
    lsls r3, r1, #16
    lsrs r3, r3, #16
    subs r2, r2, r3
    lsrs r3, r1, #16
    subs r2, r2, r3
    lsls r0, r0, #6
    lsrs r0, r0, #6
    lsls r3, r6, #26
    orrs r0, r3                @ word 0 bits 26-31 = the ability's low 6 bits
    lsls r1, r1, #3
    lsrs r1, r1, #3
    lsrs r3, r6, #6
    lsls r3, r3, #29
    orrs r1, r3                @ word 1 bits 29-31 = its high 3 bits
    lsls r3, r0, #16           @ plus the four new ones
    lsrs r3, r3, #16
    adds r2, r2, r3
    lsrs r3, r0, #16
    adds r2, r2, r3
    lsls r3, r1, #16
    lsrs r3, r3, #16
    adds r2, r2, r3
    lsrs r3, r1, #16
    adds r2, r2, r3
    strh r2, [r4, #0x1C]
    eors r0, r5
    str r0, [r7]
    eors r1, r5
    str r1, [r7, #4]
    pop {r5, r6, r7}
    pop {r3}
    bx r3
    .ltorg
    .balign 4
gpos:                          @ 0x20 + 12 x the growth substruct's place, by personality % 24
    .byte 0x20,0x20,0x20,0x20,0x20,0x20,0x2C,0x2C,0x38,0x44,0x38,0x44,0x2C,0x2C,0x38,0x44,0x38,0x44,0x2C,0x2C,0x38,0x44,0x38,0x44
table:

Any ability

A Pokémon's data has no room for an ability: it keeps a two-bit ability number (0 first, 1 second, 2 hidden) and the game looks the ability up in its species' data. But this build leaves 11 bits of the Pokémon's growth block unused, and no part of the game reads them (each was set in turn and every field the game can read compared: none changed but the checksum). So Any ability uses two parts. The party editor keeps the ability in 9 of those bits: the 6 above the held item (word 0, bits 26–31: the ability's low 6 bits) and the 3 above the EXP and the nickname's 11th letter (word 1, bits 29–31: its high 3). The routine decrypts just those two words (the key is the personality XOR the trainer ID, and the block's place comes from the personality mod 24), changes them, encrypts them again and corrects the checksum by the difference. Then the game's own SetMonData sets ability number 3, which the game never stores. The data is saved, copied and moved with the Pokémon like everything else in it.

The second code makes the game use it. Everything that needs a Pokémon's ability calls GetAbilityBySpecies (0x081CA890: the species and the ability number), which reads the species' slot and keeps the result in gLastUsedAbility (0x0200027A). For a number above 2 it takes the hidden ability, or the first non-empty one for a species without one: that's what a 3 is without the code. The code points the function's first four halfwords at a routine at 0x09FFA000 (ldr r2,[pc,#0] / bx r2 / the address). For number 3 it finds the Pokémon asking and returns the ability kept in it. The callers that matter hold the Pokémon in a register, and the routine tells them apart by their return address: GetMonAbility (0x081CA90C; the Pokémon in r5, used by the field and many battle effects), the battle's copy of each Pokémon as it comes in (0x081CB940, r5), two more in the same file (0x081CAA2A and 0x081CB518, r6) and the summary screen (0x081E5664 and 0x081E56E8: its copy of the Pokémon, at sMonSummaryScreen + 0x0C). For any other caller (a form change in battle, a menu) it takes the first Pokémon in your party, then the opponent's, of that species with number 3 and a kept ability. The kept ability is used only if it's 1–332 and that Pokémon's species is the one asked about. Anything else goes on in the game's function, so wild and trainer Pokémon are untouched.

The routine (Thumb assembly, asm/any_ability.s)
@ Generation 3 Evolved 1.0.26: "Any ability", the one universal code. Assembled at ANYAB_BASE (codes.py, 0x09FFA000,
@ in the ROM's free space); tests.py asm checks that the bytes codes.py stores still match this source.
@
@ The ability itself is kept in the Pokemon's own data: the party editor (asm/party_editor.s, field 0xFF) writes it
@ into 9 bits this build never uses, in the Pokemon's growth substruct (G): the 6 bits above the held item (word 0,
@ bits 26-31: the ability's low 6 bits) and the 3 above the EXP and the nickname's 11th character (word 1, bits 29-31:
@ its high 3 bits), and sets the ability number to 3, which the game never stores. So it is saved with the Pokemon,
@ goes into the PC, evolves with it and so on. No GetMonData field reads those bits (tested: setting each changes no
@ field but the checksum).
@
@ Every ability read goes through GetAbilityBySpecies (0x081CA890: species, ability number), which reads the species'
@ slot and keeps the result in gLastUsedAbility (0x0200027A, u16); for a number above 2 it takes the hidden ability
@ (the first non-empty one for a species without one). The code points its first four halfwords here (ldr r2,[pc,#0]
@ / bx r2 / .word). For ability number 3, this finds the Pokemon that is asking and returns the ability kept in it:
@   - its callers that hold the Pokemon: GetMonAbility (0x081CA90C; the Pokemon in r5), the battle's copy of a party
@     Pokemon (0x081CB940 in PokemonToBattleMon: r5), 0x081CAA2A and 0x081CB518 (r6), the summary screen (0x081E5664
@     and 0x081E56E8: the copy of the Pokemon at sMonSummaryScreen + 0x0C). They are told apart by the return address.
@   - any other caller (the battle's own form changes, menus): the first Pokemon in the player's party, then the
@     opponent's, of that species with ability number 3 and a kept ability.
@ The kept ability is used only if that Pokemon's species is the one asked about and it is 1-MAX; anything else (another
@ number, nothing kept) goes on exactly like the game. Untick the code and the game reads number 3 as it always would.

AnyAbility:
    lsls r2, r1, #24
    lsrs r2, r2, #24           @ the ability number (a u8, as the game reads it)
    cmp r2, #3
    bne game
    push {r0, r1, r4, r5, r6, r7, lr}
    lsls r4, r0, #16
    lsrs r4, r4, #16           @ the species
    mov r7, lr                 @ who called
    ldr r2, =RET_MON_ABILITY
    cmp r7, r2
    beq use_r5
    ldr r2, =RET_BATTLE_MON
    cmp r7, r2
    beq use_r5
    ldr r2, =RET_R6_A
    cmp r7, r2
    beq use_r6
    ldr r2, =RET_R6_B
    cmp r7, r2
    beq use_r6
    ldr r2, =RET_SUMMARY_A
    cmp r7, r2
    beq use_summary
    ldr r2, =RET_SUMMARY_B
    cmp r7, r2
    beq use_summary
    ldr r6, =gPlayerParty      @ anyone else: the player's party, then the opponent's (right after it)
    movs r5, #12
search:
    movs r0, r6
    movs r1, r4
    bl kept
    cmp r0, #0
    bne found
    adds r6, #100
    subs r5, #1
    bne search
    b notfound
use_r5:
    ldr r0, [sp, #12]          @ the caller's r5 (pushed: r0, r1, r4, r5, r6, r7, lr)
    b one
use_r6:
    ldr r0, [sp, #16]          @ the caller's r6
    b one
use_summary:
    ldr r0, =sMonSummaryScreen
    ldr r0, [r0]
    adds r0, #0x0C             @ its copy of the Pokemon
one:
    movs r1, r4
    bl kept
    cmp r0, #0
    beq notfound
found:
    ldr r1, =gLastUsedAbility
    strh r0, [r1]              @ as the game does
    add sp, #8                 @ drop the saved r0, r1
    pop {r4, r5, r6, r7}
    pop {r1}
    bx r1
notfound:
    pop {r0, r1, r4, r5, r6, r7}
    pop {r2}
    mov lr, r2
game:                          @ the four halfwords the hook replaced, then the rest of the game's function
    lsls r0, r0, #16
    lsls r1, r1, #24
    push {r4, r5, r6, r7, lr}
    lsrs r5, r0, #16
    ldr r4, =GetAbilityBySpecies_rest
    bx r4

@ kept(r0 = a Pokemon, r1 = the species asked about) -> r0 = the ability kept in it, or 0. Decrypts only the two growth
@ words and the ability number's word (the key is personality XOR OT ID; the substructs' order is personality % 24).
kept:
    push {r4, r5, r6, r7}
    movs r7, r0
    movs r6, r1
    ldr r4, [r7]               @ personality
    ldr r5, [r7, #4]
    eors r5, r4                @ the key
    lsrs r0, r4, #3
    movs r1, #3
    svc #6                     @ BIOS Div: r1 = (personality >> 3) % 3
    lsls r1, r1, #3
    lsls r4, r4, #29
    lsrs r4, r4, #29
    adds r4, r4, r1            @ personality % 24
    adr r3, offsets
    ldrb r2, [r3, r4]          @ the growth substruct's offset
    adds r3, #24
    ldrb r3, [r3, r4]          @ the offset of the misc substruct's word 8 (IVs, Egg, ability number)
    ldr r1, [r7, r3]
    eors r1, r5
    lsls r1, r1, #1
    lsrs r1, r1, #30           @ the ability number (bits 29-30)
    cmp r1, #3
    bne none
    adds r2, r7, r2
    ldr r0, [r2]
    eors r0, r5                @ growth word 0
    lsls r1, r0, #21
    lsrs r1, r1, #21           @ its species (bits 0-10)
    cmp r1, r6
    bne none
    lsrs r0, r0, #26           @ the ability's low 6 bits
    ldr r1, [r2, #4]
    eors r1, r5                @ growth word 1
    lsrs r1, r1, #29
    lsls r1, r1, #6            @ its high 3 bits
    orrs r0, r1
    beq none
    ldr r1, =MAX
    cmp r0, r1
    bls out
none:
    movs r0, #0
out:
    pop {r4, r5, r6, r7}
    bx lr
    .balign 4
offsets:                       @ 0x20 + 12 x the growth substruct's place, then 0x28 + 12 x the misc substruct's
    .byte 0x20,0x20,0x20,0x20,0x20,0x20,0x2C,0x2C,0x38,0x44,0x38,0x44,0x2C,0x2C,0x38,0x44,0x38,0x44,0x2C,0x2C,0x38,0x44,0x38,0x44
    .byte 0x4C,0x40,0x4C,0x40,0x34,0x34,0x4C,0x40,0x4C,0x40,0x34,0x34,0x4C,0x40,0x4C,0x40,0x34,0x34,0x28,0x28,0x28,0x28,0x28,0x28

EXP boost

After a win or a catch, the battle command that gives EXP (Cmd_getexp) works out each Pokémon's EXP and then calls ApplyExperienceMultipliers (0x080B687C) with a pointer to it: that adds the game's bonuses (a traded Pokémon, a Lucky Egg, the Exp. Charm's ×1.5 at 0x080B68C6–0x080B68DF), then, depending on the game's EXP option, either nothing more or its level-scaled formula. Every way out of it ends at one place, 0x080B68EA. The code turns the Exp. Charm's part into a jump past it plus a small routine (asm/exp_boost.s, in those same bytes), and the instruction at 0x080B68EA into a jump to that routine, which shifts the EXP left by 1, 2 or 3 (×2, ×4, ×8) or sets it to 457,151 for Max, caps it at 457,151, stores it and finishes the function as the game would. The cap keeps a Pokémon's EXP inside its 21-bit field: one below level 100 (the only kind that gets EXP) has less than 1,640,000, the most any growth rate needs, so it can't pass 2,097,151. The charm's ×1.5 is skipped while the code is ticked; unticking puts every byte back.

The routine (Thumb assembly, asm/exp_boost.s)
@ EXP boost (codes "EXP boost": x2 / x4 / x8 / max), Generation 3 Evolved 1.0.26 and later only.
@ Assembled at 0x080B68C4 (4-aligned, so the literal lands on a word; 0x080B68C4 itself is the game's own beq, kept), over ApplyExperienceMultipliers' own code (0x080B687C; its only caller is Cmd_getexp,
@ 0x080B720E). That function changes the EXP a party Pokemon gets from a win through a pointer (r5 = &exp). Every
@ way out of it (the plain total, the level-scaled total "exp = result + 1", and the hack's setting that skips the
@ scaling) ends at one epilogue, 0x080B68EA (pop {r6, r7} ...). This block:
@  - replaces the Exp. Charm's x1.5 (0x080B68C6-0x080B68DF: CheckBagHasItem(ITEM_EXP_CHARM) then exp * 150 / 100)
@    with a branch past it, so the charm doesn't add on top while the code is ticked;
@  - puts the boost in the bytes that frees: exp << SHIFT (or LIMIT for max), capped at LIMIT, stored back, then the
@    epilogue's first instruction and a branch back to the rest of it;
@  - makes the epilogue's first instruction (0x080B68EA) branch to the boost.
@ The bytes between (0x080B68E0-0x080B68E9: the scaling setting check) are the game's own, unchanged.
@ LIMIT = 457,151: EXP is stored in a 21-bit field (2,097,151 at most) that the game doesn't cap when it adds a win's
@ EXP, and a Pokemon below level 100 (the only ones that get EXP) has less than 1,640,000 (the most any growth rate
@ needs for level 100), so with at most 457,151 per win the field can't wrap around.
@ Symbols: BOOST (lsls r0, r0, #n for x2 / x4 / x8, or ldr r0, limit for max) is chosen in codes.py.
    .hword  0xD047              @ 0x080B68C4: the game's own beq, unchanged
    b       charm_done          @ 0x080B68C6: skip the Exp. Charm's x1.5
boost:                          @ 0x080B68C8
    ldr     r0, [r5]
    BOOST
    ldr     r1, limit
    cmp     r0, r1
    bls     store
    movs    r0, r1
store:
    str     r0, [r5]
    pop     {r6, r7}            @ the epilogue's first instruction (replaced at 0x080B68EA)
    b       epilogue_rest
    nop
    .balign 4
limit:
    .word   457151
charm_done:                     @ 0x080B68E0: the game's own bytes from here (the level-scaling setting check)
    .hword  0x200A, 0xF043, 0xFFD9, 0x2803, 0xD844
    b       boost               @ 0x080B68EA (was pop {r6, r7})
epilogue_rest:                  @ 0x080B68EC: mov sb, r7 ...

Eggs hatch fast

On each step the game's Day Care code (0x0810D7C0) counts, and once every 128 steps (cmp r3,#0x7F at 0x0810D806) it takes egg cycles off each Egg in the party: one, or two with a Flame Body Pokémon in the party (0x08126BA8). The cycles are kept in the Egg's friendship field, and an Egg with none left hatches. Fast makes that compare 0, so it happens on every step: a Bulbasaur Egg (20 cycles) hatched within 22 steps, within 12 with Flame Body. At once also sets the cycles straight to 0 (0x0810D84E and 0x0810D852), so it hatches within the first few steps. This is the same change as Wishes of Tomorrow's, at 1.0.26's addresses.

Raw forms

At once

00000000 18086C03 00002B00 00000000 00000000 18086C27 00002300 00000000 00000000 18086C29 0000E000 00000000

00000000 18086C03 00002B00 00000000

Berries come back after battle

As a battle starts, the game notes each party Pokémon's held item (gBattleStruct + 0x378, one entry per slot). When it's over, the battle's last step (0x0808BCD4) calls TryRestoreHeldItems (0x080D48D8), the expansion's routine, built here with its Gen 9 setting: every lost item comes back, but not a berry. For each slot it takes the item noted. If that item is in the Berries pocket (GetItemPocket, 0x08171210), it reads what the Pokémon holds now: the same berry means nothing to do; anything else clears the noted item, and the routine then writes "nothing" in a trainer battle (that's how the game gives back an item your Pokémon took from the trainer) and leaves the slot alone in a wild one. Just before the write, a second pocket check refuses any berry. The write is the game's SetMonData (0x081C6E48), which re-encrypts the Pokémon and fixes its checksum.

The code changes four halfwords of that routine. The second pocket check (beq at 0x080D492C) becomes a nop, so a berry can be written. In the berry branch (0x080D495C–0x080D4960), movs r3,#0 / movs r0,#0 / strh r3,[r6] (clear the noted item) become cmp r3,#0 / beq 0x080D4966 / nop: the noted berry is kept, and when the Pokémon holds nothing it goes straight to the write. When it holds something else, the game's own rule decides: the write happens only in a trainer battle, now with the berry where the game wrote "nothing". A Pokémon that kept its berry has the same berry written again, which changes nothing. Items that aren't berries go the game's way, as before. Unticking puts the four halfwords back.

Raw forms
00000000 1806A496 000046C0 00000000 00000000 1806A4AE 00002B00 00000000 00000000 1806A4AF 0000D002 00000000 00000000 1806A4B0 000046C0 00000000

Tested

Tested on 1.0.26 in mGBA 0.10.5, through the real Start menu (Start → POKéMON) like a player. Each edited Pokémon was read back: every field the code set, its stats against the game's stat formula (with the nature it uses for stats), the ability the game uses (GetMonAbility), and every field it must not touch, field by field, from the personality and moves to the ribbons and where it was met (a self-check flips each of those 672 bits in turn and confirms the comparison catches every one). The safeguard tests give it the wrong species, a reordered party, an empty slot and an Egg of the right species, and check that nothing changes. Edits are made to the lead and to a Pokémon received with the game's own givemon; some open the menu twice, some end with save → untick → reset → Continue. One run ticks it together with every other code on the page (all but No random encounters, since that run needs a wild battle). The Any ability tests read the ability the game uses (GetMonAbility, the summary screen, and the battle's own copy of each Pokémon with its stat stages: Intimidate has to lower the foe's Attack as it comes in), check it after evolving, after save → untick → reset → Continue and after the game's own writes to the Pokémon, give two species (Articuno, Zapdos) and two of one species different abilities, and call GetAbilityBySpecies for every species and ability number with and without the code. The EXP tests win a real battle (the lead, a Lv5 Charmander, given 999 HP, Attack and Speed in its party data so it wins with one move, against a Lv30 Kecleon with 1 HP) or catch one, and read the EXP the game gives. The egg tests give an Egg with giveegg and walk (with No random encounters ticked), counting steps. Each ends with save → untick → reset → Continue where it says so.

Berries come back after battle: the battles played

Real battles in mGBA from the test save (Route 1, a Lv5 Charmander), started with the game's own script commands: setwildbattle / dowildbattle for wild battles (single and double) and trainerbattle for a trainer's (one Mightyena). Moves are picked from the battle menu. Test-only changes in the emulator's memory: the moves in each battler's battle data set to the one the run needs, our Pokémon's battle stats raised so it moves first and lives, its HP lowered to make it eat an Oran Berry, and the foe's HP set to 1 to win. Each battle is played twice from the same moment: with no code, then with the code. Each row says what was held after it both times, and every party Pokémon had to be the same, byte for byte, both times except for the held item. The partial runs tick one of the four halfwords at a time (as if the code were ticked while the game is in that routine). The save run ends with save → untick → reset → Continue and checks the money, coins, Bag, flags, variables and place against the same run without the code.

9E6E6246 4AE23266 935EC117 F2B7918F

Makes the TM you pick teach the move you pick. Choose both here, or press TM code on any move in the list below. While it's ticked, the Bag lists the TM as the new move and teaches it.

  • Who can learn it goes by the move: a Pokémon that can't learn the new move can't learn it from this TM either (tick Any Pokémon can learn any TM too for that).
  • TMs aren't used up in this game, so you can teach the new move as often as you like.
  • What a Pokémon learned stays after you untick it.

11F0530E 0AE4265F AD86124F 2823D8DA AC38E656 947301C0 65DA389A 09D8882C F89677D1 49984F66 AD86124F 2823D8DA C3DE24B7 258EA056 65DA389A 09D8882C

While it's ticked, any Pokémon can learn any TM or HM from the Bag, and any move a move tutor teaches.

  • An Egg, or a Pokémon that already knows the move, still can't.
  • A Pokémon that already knows four moves forgets one, as usual.
  • What a Pokémon learned stays after you untick it.

Every move

All 855 moves you can learn in the game, with their type, power, accuracy, PP and the game's own description, and which TM or HM teaches each one (and whether a move tutor does). — for power means the move doesn't deal damage or works its damage out itself; for accuracy, that it never misses. The descriptions are the game's.

Under the hoodHow the TM codes work, how they were tested, and how the move list was read

How each code works

The raw forms below are 1.0.24's (the TM template follows the version picked). The addresses in the text are 1.0.20's, where the codes were first worked out: in 1.0.24 the same code sits up to 0x340 bytes further on, and every address the 1.0.24 codes use is listed on the Start here tab (Under the hood, "Version 1.0.24").

The raw forms below are 1.0.26's (the TM template follows the version picked). The addresses in the text are 1.0.20's, where the codes were first worked out: in 1.0.26 the same code sits 0x94 to 0x780 bytes further on, and every address the 1.0.26 codes use is listed on the Start here tab (Under the hood, "Version 1.0.26").

Change what a TM teaches

00000000 183E05AA 0000MMMM 00000000

The game looks up each TM's move in a table at 0x087C0624, 2 bytes per TM and HM item (TM01 is item 582, TM100 is 681, HM01 is 682, HM09 is 690), through ItemIdToBattleMoveId (0x081AF96C). The first line of the code points at the chosen TM's 2 bytes and the second line is the move ID to put there. While the code is ticked, mGBA writes that move ID into its copy of the game; when you untick it, mGBA puts the original 2 bytes back. The Bag and the teaching read that one entry, which is why they change together. Who can learn it is checked against the move itself (the species' list of teachable moves), so it follows the new move.

MMMM is the move's 4-digit hex ID from the list; the first line depends on the TM picked in the card.

Any Pokémon can learn any TM

00000000 1807CAC4 00002001 00000000 00000000 1807CAC5 000046C0 00000000 00000000 1807CBF0 00002001 00000000 00000000 1807CBF1 000046C0 00000000
00000000 1807CB90 00002001 00000000 00000000 1807CB91 000046C0 00000000 00000000 1807CCBC 00002001 00000000 00000000 1807CCBD 000046C0 00000000

This game teaches TMs and the move tutors' moves through the Pokémon storage screen: the move goes in var 0x8005 and the screen (ChooseBoxMon, 0x080F941C) marks who can learn it with a check (0x080F9340) that refuses an Egg or a Pokémon that knows the move, and otherwise asks CanLearnTeachableMove (0x081CCABC; the bl at 0x080F936C). The teaching itself asks again (the bl at 0x080F95C4) before it teaches. The code turns both calls into movs r0, #1 / nop: "yes". No species' data is edited, and the move relearner and the battle code, which use the same check, aren't touched.

The tests below ran on 1.0.20; switch the version at the top of the page for 1.0.24 and 1.0.26's.

Tested

The TM tests go through the Bag and the storage screen exactly like a player: Start → Bag → TMs & HMs → the TM → Use → the messages → Yes → the storage screen → Party Pokémon → the Pokémon → Select. The tutor tests open the same screen the way the game's move tutors do (ChooseBoxMon, selection 3, with the move in var 0x8005).

How the list and the learnsets were read

0x087A7738
Move table (gMovesInfo): 72 bytes per move. Name +0, description pointer +4, type, category and power +0xA (bits 0–4, 5–6 and 7–15), accuracy +0xC (bits 0–6; 0: never misses), PP +0xE, priority +0x10 (bits 0–3, signed). 855 moves; the Z-Moves and Max Moves after them are battle-only and left out of the list. Type names: gTypesInfo (0x0846C9D8).
Level-up
gSpeciesInfo +0x94: a pointer per species to {u16 move, u16 level} pairs, ending with move 0xFFFF.
TMs, HMs and tutors
gSpeciesInfo +0x98: a pointer per species to the moves it can be taught (ending with 0xFFFF). This game's CanLearnTeachableMove (0x081CCABC) also has rules of its own for a few moves, by a species' types and stats (any Ghost type can learn Shadow Sneak, for instance), which add Psychic Fangs, Ice Shard, Shadow Sneak, Ominous Wind, Power Gem and Flash Freeze to 338 species beyond their lists. So the page's lists are the game's own answers: the function was run in mGBA for every species and every move. The ones on a TM or HM are listed as TMs, the rest as tutor moves (170 moves).
Egg moves
gSpeciesInfo +0x9C: a pointer per species to its egg moves, ending with 0xFFFF.

The level-up lists were checked against the game itself: a Pokémon given at level 100 and at level 25 starts with the last four moves its list gives up to its level (the game skips the level-0 moves, which are learned on evolving). The teachable lists were checked again on a random sample, one call per move.

Every ability

All 331 abilities in the game, with the game's own description and how many species have each one (click the number to see them in Pokédex). Most species have three: a first, a second and a hidden one. To give a new Pokémon its first, second or hidden ability, use Pick the ability on the Catching tab.

Under the hoodWhere the list comes from and how it was checked

Where the data comes from

The look-ups on this page are read from 1.0.26, the latest, and the tests below ran on it (the same tests passed on 1.0.20 and 1.0.24). Where the older versions differ: nowhere on this tab (the abilities and each species' abilities are the same in all three). The addresses here are 1.0.20's, where this guide was first worked out; each is at most 0x780 bytes further on in 1.0.26.

0x08D7B3EC
gAbilitiesInfo (pointed at by the expansion's ROM header, +0x10): 28 bytes per ability, the name at +0 and a pointer to its description at +0x14, the line the summary screen shows under the name. Entry 0 ("-------") is no ability.
0x087CA828
gSpeciesInfo, 0x108 bytes per species: the first, second and hidden ability at +0x18, +0x1A and +0x1C (1280 species have a hidden one).
0x081CA4E0
GetAbilityBySpecies, which turns a species and an ability number (0 first, 1 second, 2 hidden) into the ability; an empty slot falls back to the first.

Tested

The game's own GetAbilityBySpecies was called for every species and all three ability numbers and compared with the list.
